	June 21, 2025
		(Source:  https://aisera.com/blog/llm-embeddings/#llm-embeddings-from-one-hot-encoding-to-generative-pretrained-transformers )                       

		NLP studying: (For the Cisco Senior NLP job)                       
			Explain the attention mechanism.    
				The attention mechanism trains the neural network to absorb meaning from the most important parts of the sentences.  ie. given the sentence "the quick brown dog ran from the fox," the most relevant words for the meaning of the sentence are "dog, ran, from, [and] fox."  After having spoken the English language our whole lives, this is very very intuitive to people.                 
					This is in contrast to sequential processing mechanisms which forgets older information much quicker.  Attention allows the network to remember the most relevant information and weight THAT as more important than just the most recent words.                           
						But shouldn't the sequential position of the word(s) and/or images (in the video case) matter for understanding as well?                  

			Explain encoding and decoding neural networks.
				An encoding architecture most generally takes a large input and reduces the dimensionality of it to a very very small output size.  For instance, a large corpus of text on Wikipedia could be reduced to "a lot of educational information about the website Neopets."                      
				The decoder architecture then takes these embeddings, which are less like words and more like encoded sequences of numbers, and creates a large output, in this case a different educational information about the website Neopets.                               
                               
			Explain embeddings.                               
				Embeddings are numerical representations of data (like words, sentences, or even images) that capture their semantic meaning and relationships.  The tensors of numbers have meaning in the context of that particular neural network, which gets used in later parts of the network, both in the training and inference steps.                              

			Multimodal embeddings are like for video processing; the images and spoken words are multiple modes of data that are associated with each other to capture a richer understanding an meaning than an individual medium      .                               



					ie. They communicate the meaning of the word in the sentence, the part of speech it is, etc.                               
			Explain tokenization.                                    
				"Tokenization" means the neural network splits an input into multiple, smaller tokens before processing it further.  A great example is in python, using `"This is a sentence".split(" ")` yields each word individually.  More complex and strategic tokenization would handle other tokens like periods and commas separately, ie. ["This", "is", "a", "sentence", ",", "and", "that", "is", "a", "Stegosaurus", "."]                                   

			Explain the pre-training phase.    :
				Pre-training comes before the model is trained on a specific task.  The model learns a more general understanding of the problem space, and generally "levels up" its IQ.                              
			Explain the transfer learning phase.                  
				The pre-trained model is fine-tuned on the exact task, training data, answer(s), and  .                                

			Explain the transformer architecture.		(And/or the attention mechanism)      
				T

			Explain one-hot encoding and how it is worse than meaning embeddings through GPT, etc.        
				TF-IDF (Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency) is better than one-hot encoding because it accounts for the meanings of the words, rather than just saying "a king is as related to a queen as either is to a hadron".)                        
					TF-IDF   represents words based on their frequency in a document compared to their frequency across all documents. While it captures some semantic information, it falls short in capturing syntactic relationships and context.                           
				Word2Vec is another algorithm that is similar, and it encodes the meaning(s) of words into their vector representation much better than a one-hot encoding would.                              
			Various more successful embedding schemes:
				Advanced embedding techniques like Word2Vec, GloVe, and FastText have significantly enhanced word representation. Word2Vec captures semantic and syntactic relationships based on word co-occurrence. GloVe combines co-occurrence information with direct context prediction, while FastText, an extension of Word2Vec, captures the meaning of shorter words and affixes effectively.
					FastText is the best out of those options.  But GPT-2 [was/is] using Glove??       BERT is supposed to be better.                               
						BERT

					Unlike Word2Vec, which focuses on local context windows, GloVe considers the entire corpus to build a co-occurrence matrix. 
						        f
						        f
						        f
	===================================================================================================
					Gemini, explain GloVe, direct context, and co-occurrrence   :                               
					"""
						
					GloVe is an unsupervised learning algorithm that generates vector representations (embeddings) for words.    It does not use neural networks, which makes it more lightweight than BERT or Word2Vec.    					
					  It does this by mapping words into a meaningful space where the distance between words is related to their semantic similarity.
					  GloVe leverages global word-word co-occurrence statistics from a corpus, considering how frequently words appear together across the entire dataset.
					It's a count-based model that uses matrix factorization techniques on a word-context matrix.
					  Unlike Word2Vec, which focuses on local context windows, GloVe considers the entire corpus to build a co-occurrence matrix.
					Direct Context
					  In NLP, "direct context" usually refers to the words immediately surrounding a target word within a defined window size.
					  Word2Vec, for instance, trains by considering this local context, predicting surrounding words given a target word or vice-versa.
					  The "context window" size is the number of words before and after a center word that are considered part of its context.

					  NEARBY words.   
					Co-occurrence
					  Co-occurrence, in this context, refers to the frequency with which two words appear together within a corpus.
					  A co-occurrence matrix is created, where each entry represents the number of times a word appears in the context of another word.
					  GloVe focuses on capturing the global statistics of word co-occurrences by analyzing this matrix.

						Within a LARGER document.   
					"""

					GPT, for instance, infuses its embeddings with positional encodings, ensuring the order of words is preserved. As the input text passes through the transformer layers of GPT, the embeddings evolve, absorbing more context and enriching their representation. By the end, what emerges is a vector representation for each token that is deeply contextualized, reflecting not just the token itself but its relationship with every other token in the sequence.


		BERT, GPT, etc. based embeddings:
			However, these precise embeddings come at the cost of increased memory usage and CPU consumption. This challenges the efficiency of LLM models. Simpler techniques like one-hot encoding or TF-IDF are less resource-intensive but lack the depth and context of more sophisticated embeddings. Businesses must weigh the benefits of richer, contextual embeddings against the computational costs, especially in large-scale AI applications where efficiency is critical.
			
			From "Attention Is All You Need" (the original transformer paper)     (https://arxiv.org/pdf/1706.03762):        "Similarly to other sequence transduction models, we use learned embeddings to convert the input tokens and output tokens to vectors of dimension d_{model}. W"

ChatGPT's study guide:

Cisco NLP interview prep
June 21, 2025

Hey ChatGPT! I am preparing for a job interview through Google Colaboratory. It is a Natural Language Processing job, and I only have this weekend to prepare and show off my PyTorch skills. How can I review and get fluent in the material as fast as possible? Thanks!! Warmly, Nathan

ChatGPT said:

Hey Nathan!
That’s exciting! Preparing for a Natural Language Processing (NLP) role at Google is a great opportunity. With the weekend crunch, let’s focus on maximizing your time and covering key concepts in a structured way so you can showcase your skills confidently in PyTorch.
Here’s a fast, focused approach to review and practice your NLP skills with PyTorch:
1. Set Clear Goals for the Weekend
Deepen Core NLP concepts (Embeddings, attention, transformers, tokenization, etc.)


Solidify your PyTorch workflow (data pipelines, model building, and training)


Prepare a Portfolio Project (Implement a small but impactful NLP task, such as text classification, named entity recognition, or sentiment analysis)

===================================================================================================
	2. Structured Study Plan



You need a balance of theory and hands-on coding. Here's a tight breakdown for you:
Day 1: Solidify Foundations
Morning: NLP Concepts Refresher


Preprocessing and Tokenization: Learn about tokenization, stemming, lemmatization, and text cleaning.


Word Embeddings: Review word2vec, GloVe, and FastText. Try implementing a simple embedding layer in PyTorch.


Text Classification Basics: Familiarize yourself with models like Naive Bayes, RNNs, CNNs, and LSTMs for text classification.


PyTorch Basics:


Brush up on tensors, autograd, and neural networks.


Go through the PyTorch docs to understand tensor operations and model setup.


Practice:


Implement a simple text classification model using LSTM or CNN.


Use a dataset like IMDb for sentiment analysis.


Day 2: Dive Into Advanced Techniques
Morning: Transformers & Attention Mechanisms


Self-Attention and Multi-Head Attention: Understand the importance of attention layers in NLP.


Transformers: Study the architecture of the Transformer model. Review the Encoder-Decoder structure and how it powers models like BERT and GPT.


PyTorch Advanced:


Learn about the nn.Transformer class in PyTorch, or try implementing your own from scratch (great for interviews).


Practice using HuggingFace Transformers to fine-tune a pre-trained model.


Practice:


BERT for Text Classification or Named Entity Recognition (NER) using the HuggingFace library and fine-tuning on a custom dataset.


Day 3: Portfolio Project + Interview Prep
Morning: Build a Complete NLP Pipeline


Design a small but complete NLP pipeline:


Dataset loading: Load and preprocess a text dataset (e.g., IMDB reviews, SST-2, or a custom dataset).


Model creation: Build a model in PyTorch. If you’re feeling ambitious, use a pre-trained transformer model.


Training: Train the model, tune hyperparameters, and evaluate performance.


Interview Prep:


Review common NLP interview questions related to PyTorch, such as:


How does backpropagation work in PyTorch?


How would you deploy a trained NLP model for production?


What’s the difference between RNNs, LSTMs, and GRUs?


How do you deal with large-scale text data?


3. Useful Resources
PyTorch Documentation: Always useful for a quick refresher.


HuggingFace Docs: Learn how to fine-tune transformers like BERT, GPT, etc.


Kaggle: They have many pre-built notebooks. Look at top-rated notebooks on NLP problems to see common practices.




4. Final Touches (Day 3)
Try explaining concepts aloud as if you were in the interview.


Mock Interviews: Try practicing with friends or online platforms like Pramp or Interviewing.io.


Bonus Tips
Use Google Colab to easily run your models, as it's optimized for quick testing and has free GPU access.


If you're time-limited, focus on one project and make sure it's polished. It’s better to have one complete and well-implemented project than several half-baked ones.


Project Suggestion: Text Classification with BERT
Use HuggingFace’s Transformers library to load a pre-trained model (like BERT or RoBERTa).


Fine-tune on a sentiment analysis or text classification task (like IMDB or SST-2).


Metrics: Evaluate with accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score.


Prepare a short demo script showcasing model performance and your understanding of transfer learning.



Does this strategy sound good? Or would you like to dive into specific concepts, libraries, or tasks? Let me know how I can help you further!

	Performance review:

			Overall impact                     
			Both the technical abilities and the team leading process

		No PIPs, etc.

	Lessons learned:    (AKA Postmortem)                     
		Review the main 10 areas of LeetCode  before a LeetCode interview, NO MATTER WHAT.       (Heaps, DP, Recursion, BFS & DFS, Hash Tables, Deques, Binary Search,  Basic Sorting, Sliding Window, Backtracking, Two Pointers,              Djikstra's,  etc.    [see LeetCode's topic bubbles for more])                       
  		Always get enough sleep the night before,   
		If you lie, lie only **__VERY__** superficially and .  Stick to the areas you know best.  Small lies don't get caught.  Big lies do.       
		Get a clear plan of your path forward BEFORE you start writing code.

	cs224n: word vectors and "embeddings" are the used synonymously, interchangeably, even though they don't mean exactly the same thing.       


	SVD :     (https://davetang.org/file/Singular_Value_Decomposition_Tutorial.pdf)                     
		At a high level, SVD being like PCA makes a lot of sense to me.  But the formulation that  makes more sense to me:           
 			In essence, SVD decomposes any matrix into a combination of rotations (U and V), and scaling (Σ). The singular values in Σ determine the scaling factors along the directions defined by the columns of U and V."
				makes more sense to me.  It sounds pretty similar to eigenvalues and eigenvectors that Grant Sanderson explained...      



The full explanation:


The Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) of a matrix A is expressed by the equation A = UΣVT, where U and V are orthogonal matrices, and Σ is a diagonal matrix with non-negative singular values. 
Here's a breakdown:
A:     This is the original matrix that we want to decompose. It can be a rectangular matrix (m x n). 
U:     This is an m x m orthogonal matrix, also known as the left singular vectors. Its columns are the eigenvectors of AAT. 
Σ:     This is an m x n diagonal matrix containing the singular values of A. The singular values are the square roots of the eigenvalues of AAT or ATA. They are arranged in descending order along the diagonal. 
V T: This is the transpose of an n x n orthogonal matrix V, also known as the right singular vectors. Its columns are the eigenvectors of ATA. 
In essence, SVD decomposes any matrix into a combination of rotations (U and V), and scaling (Σ). The singular values in Σ determine the scaling factors along the directions defined by the columns of U and V. 



Another great, simple, more intuitive explanation for NLP:  
	"What makes SVD practical for NLP applications is that you can simply ignore variation below a particular threshhold to massively reduce your data but be assured that the main"                     

Yet another helpful formulation of SVD:
	"the third way of viewing SVD, which is that once we have identified where the most variation is, it’s possible to find the best approximation of the original data points using fewer dimensions. Hence, SVD can be seen as a method for data reduction."   




So SVD is used with GloVe in the co-occurrence matrices to find the dimensions of most variance?

DataAnnotation exam answers:    

	1.  There is a for-each loop in the Solution B.  The bug in Solution A is it fails to generalize for arrays longer than 3 elements long, and also will throw an ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException when the code tries to access elements arr[2] for arrays of length 0, 1, or 2.  And Solution C won't even compile at all, because word1, word2, and word3 are not declared as arr[0], arr[1], and arr[2].  It also has the same issue as Solution A, that it doesn't check for all the strings in the array.




	2.  There are a few reasons Function A is the best.  Let's first start with Function B, the worst function; Function B is extremely inefficient because it A) generates and checks all the numbers from 2 to n-1, rather than 2 to sqrt(n), like Function A, which is all that is necessary to check, and B) Function B is implemented with an array of numbers of memory efficiency O(n), and the solution also removes i from the list *as it is being processed*, each operation for which adds additional processing time.  Function B does not need to generate an O(n) array at all; Function A's solution uses only O(1) space complexity because all we need to keep track of is the current value of i: a single number rather than an array of N numbers.  Function C is also less efficient than Function A, but more efficient than Function B because at LEAST it doesn't use the array of numbers of Function B.  Function C also has the issue I labeled "A)" in the second sentence; we only need to check a max of sqrt(n) numbers


	3.   (see screenshot of the order to put the code in)   


	4. 	There are many reasons to prefer Response A.  Response A helps protect the user from legal, financial, and emotional troubles due to their nefarious password-stealing desires.  Our duty is to protect the user from themselves.  Additionally, there is a moral, legal, and financial obligation to the company we work for; if we give out illegal information like Response B, we could be sued, get bad press, and/or be criminally prosecuted for aiding in federal misdemeanors and felonies.  As an employee with a moral compass, it is my job to protect the user and company from accessing that information.  A disclaimer like the AI provides is not enough to stop the user from stealing their neighbors' wi-fi passwords; we should absolutely prevent them from being able to do so



	5.	First, let me just point out that the *code* generated in both responses are *exactly* equal.  However, due to the explanations the AI provides, I prefer Response B.  Response B's explanation is more intuitive to humans, and helps add *extra, simple, intuitive meaning* to the code that the user would not get just by reading the literal code.  ie. that description reads more like a comment then the left side, which is more like hand-tracing.  Response B is also shorter.

To be a bit balanced, Response A does at least walk through the steps with a concrete example.  Hand-tracing an entire example can definitely help the programmer understand and visualize what the code does, whereas Response B does not do that.  But in this case, the user already provided an example with some hand-tracing, so clearly the programmer already understood the problem pretty well.  So I prefer Response B



	Code for the Google Docs scraping assessment:   

"""
Recursively extracts the text from a Google Doc.
"""
import googleapiclient.discovery as discovery
from httplib2 import Http
from oauth2client import client
from oauth2client import file
from oauth2client import tools

SCOPES = 'https://www.googleapis.com/auth/documents.readonly'
DISCOVERY_DOC = 'https://docs.googleapis.com/$discovery/rest?version=v1'
DOCUMENT_ID = 'YOUR_DOCUMENT_ID'

def get_credentials():
  """Gets valid user credentials from storage.

  If nothing has been stored, or if the stored credentials are invalid,
  the OAuth 2.0 flow is completed to obtain the new credentials.

  Returns:
      Credentials, the obtained credential.
  """
  store = file.Storage('token.json')
  credentials = store.get()

  if not credentials or credentials.invalid:
    flow = client.flow_from_clientsecrets('credentials.json', SCOPES)
    credentials = tools.run_flow(flow, store)
  return credentials


def add_current_and_child_tabs(tab, all_tabs):
  """Adds the provided tab to the list of all tabs, and recurses through and
  adds all child tabs.

  Args:
      tab: a Tab from a Google Doc.
      all_tabs: a list of all tabs in the document.
  """
  all_tabs.append(tab)
  for tab in tab.get('childTabs'):
    add_current_and_child_tabs(tab, all_tabs)


def get_all_tabs(doc):
  """Returns a flat list of all tabs in the document in the order they would
  appear in the UI (top-down ordering). Includes all child tabs.

  Args:
      doc: a document.
  """
  all_tabs = []
  # Iterate over all tabs and recursively add any child tabs to generate a
  # flat list of Tabs.
  for tab in doc.get('tabs'):
    add_current_and_child_tabs(tab, all_tabs)
  return all_tabs


def read_paragraph_element(element):
  """Returns the text in the given ParagraphElement.

  Args:
      element: a ParagraphElement from a Google Doc.
  """
  text_run = element.get('textRun')
  if not text_run:
    return ''
  return text_run.get('content')


def read_structural_elements(elements):
  """Recurses through a list of Structural Elements to read a document's text
  where text may be in nested elements.

  Args:
      elements: a list of Structural Elements.
  """
  text = ''
  for value in elements:
    if 'paragraph' in value:
      elements = value.get('paragraph').get('elements')
      for elem in elements:
        text += read_paragraph_element(elem)
    elif 'table' in value:
      # The text in table cells are in nested Structural Elements and tables may
      # be nested.
      table = value.get('table')
      for row in table.get('tableRows'):
        cells = row.get('tableCells')
        for cell in cells:
          text += read_structural_elements(cell.get('content'))
    elif 'tableOfContents' in value:
      # The text in the TOC is also in a Structural Element.
      toc = value.get('tableOfContents')
      text += read_structural_elements(toc.get('content'))
  return text


def main():
  """Uses the Docs API to print out the text of a document."""
  credentials = get_credentials()
  http = credentials.authorize(Http())
  docs_service = discovery.build(
      'docs', 'v1', http=http, discoveryServiceUrl=DISCOVERY_DOC
  )
  # Fetch the document with all of the tabs populated, including any nested
  # child tabs.
  doc = (
      docs_service.documents()
      .get(documentId=DOCUMENT_ID, include_tabs_content=True)
      .execute()
  )
  all_tabs = get_all_tabs(doc)

  # Print the text from each tab in the document.
  for tab in all_tabs:
    # Get the DocumentTab from the generic Tab.
    document_tab = tab.get('documentTab')
    doc_content = document_tab.get('body').get('content')
    print(read_structural_elements(doc_content))


def parse_google_doc(url):
  f






if __name__ == '__main__':
  main()

  # Test
  parse_google_doc("https://docs.google.com/document/d/1Z8Ku1riLzMPrtXr7f5IkrLTJVrfib5N5aMwHbDi7ELw/edit?usp=sharing")

	      August 12,    2025                 
  =================================         
        Notes on Databricks:     		(from the questions at "https://www.datacamp.com/blog/databricks-interview-questions")                     

		  1. What is Databricks, and what are its key features?   
        	Databricks is a data analytics platform known for its collaborative notebooks, its Spark engine, and its data lakes, such a Delta Lake which has ACID transactions.  Databricks also, of course, integrates with various data sources and BI tools, and offers good security features.   
				What is ACID?     
				What are data lakes, besides being similar ish to databases?    
				What are BI tools? 			"BI" stands for "Business Intelligence (BI)," and is similar(-ish) to Matplotlib and other data visualization tools                     
					"software applications that help organizations collect, analyze, and visualize data to support business decision-making"    (Similar-ish to matplotlib)        
					ie. Tableau and PowerBI                      

          2.  Explain the core architecture of Databricks.            
                        
         	The core architecture of Databricks is made up of a few key parts.  First, there's the Databricks Runtime, which includes essential components like Spark that run on a cluster.  Then, there are the clusters themselves, which are scalable compute resources used for running notebooks and jobs.  Notebooks in Databricks are (basically Jupyter notebooks) interactive documents that mix code, visualizations, and text.  The workspace is where you organize and manage these notebooks, as well as libraries and experiments.  Lastly, there's the Databricks File System, which is a distributed file system that's attached to the clusters.                                        
                 
       	  3.  How do you create and run a notebook in Databricks?                        
        	Creating and running a notebook in Databricks is straightforward.  First, go to the Databricks workspace where you want to create your notebook.  Click on "Create" and choose "Notebook."  Give your notebook a name and select the default language, ie. Python, and attach it to a cluster.  Then, to run your notebook, simply write or paste your code into a cell, and click the "run" button (the keyboard shortcut for which is "Ctrl+Enter" or "Command+Enter" on a Mac.        
                
		TO-DOs:   Data Lakes,                             
			Data Lakes,                                     
			What is ACID?  What are its practical benefits for data lakes?                                          
				"ACID" stands for "Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, and Durability" .                      
					"Atomicity" means that all transactions either succeed or fail completely.                         
					"Consistency" guarantees relate to how a given state of the data is observed by simultaneous operations.                     
					"Isolation" refers to how simultaneous operations potentially conflict with one another.                           
					"Durability" means that committed changes are permanent.                     
                
	==================================================================
				"ETL" stands for "Extract, Transform, Load" .  It is a process in data warehousing and data integration where data is extracted from various source systems, transformed into a usable format, and then loaded into a target system like a data warehouse.          

===================================================================================================



===================================================================================================
Notes on the Databricks fundamentals tutorial(s)   Lessons from the   :                    
	( https://www.databricks.com/learn/training/databricks-fundamentals#data-video )     

Databricks Fundamentals
  Title of sub-section: 							Time it will take:         

	1. The Databricks Story 						10 minutes                  
	2. Data Lakehouses and Data Intelligence		10 minutes
	3. Databricks Architecture						10 minutes
	4. Databricks Security and Governance			 5 minutes
	5. Databricks Products and Features				10 minutes
  ===================================================================================================
Course Learning Objectives:                   
	Differentiate between data warehouses, data lakes, data lakehouses, and data intelligence platforms.
	Recognize how Databricks fits within your cloud data ecosystem.
	Describe how Databricks supports security and governance needs for any industry.
	List the products and features Databricks offers for different data-centric needs.

"Get Started Onboarding" course is better for the technical details of Databricks.  I can go through that one after I finish this one.                         

1. The Databricks story:
	Databricks is the name of a company as well as a platform.                              


	Data is everywhere and everyone needs to leverage it.
		Basically every company wants to be a Data + AI company today.   
	Leaders are seeing the benefits:  
		1.  CFOs seek:     Reduced TCO to enable other investments                         
		2.	Data quality and security:		High quality data that is secure and consumable                  
		3.  Transformational AI for their organization and business                      

	The data estate is fragmented:
		It's a complexity nightmare of *high costs* and *proprietary formats*                      
			Governance, GenAI, Orchestration and ETL, Data Lake, BI, ...,   etc.                  
		This complexity slows down success:                  
			1.  Reduced TCO to enable other investments:                         (the success slower:    Costs grow across many platforms (redundant, expensive, etc.  ))                                                  
			2.  Data quality and security:		High quality data that is secure and consumable                 (the success slower:  it is hard to maintain a source of truth for the business and compliance)                         
			3.  Transformational AI for their organization and business      	(the success slower: Most AI projects don't make it to production)

	Data warehouses vs. lakehouses vs.    ...

	External Data, &    Operational Data                    Structured, Semi-Structured, and Unstructured Data                       
===================================================================================================

Let's consider both.  Data Warehouses are the older of the two.                          


   Data warehouses:    (the older of the two)    

	Strengths:			Structured data, ie. tables.                     
		- Purpose-built for BI (Business Intelligence) and reporting
		- Meant to unify disparate systems		
		- The data warehouses house structured, clean data with a standardized schema   

	Downsides:
		- No support for semi or unstructured data
		- Poor support to data science, AI and streaming use cases
		- Uses closed and proprietary formats
		- Expensive to scale



	vs.   

   Data lakes:

	Strengths:			
		- Unstructured or semi-structured data are allowed IN ADDITION TO structured data.      (no schema necessary)                     
		- Store any kind of data		
		- Inexpensive storage
		- Good starting point
		- Support for GenAI and streaming use cases

	Downsides:			
		- Complex to set up   (esp for employees more familiar with setting up warehouses)   
		- Poor BI performance  (invalidated and dirty)   
		- Can become unreliable data swamps    (again, because it's invalidated and dirty)   
		- Governance concerns
		- Warehouses still needed   


	So why don't we combine the ideas and create a lakehouse?  Historically, this was difficult because 		(just see the fucking diagram in video 2; using ASCII art makes it so much more unclear than just being able to use Powerpoint like normal)   


			Data Warehouses												Data Lakes

	Business Intelligence	& 	SQL Analytics (Data warehouses)
	  1. Incomplete support for use cases 							Data Science & ML     Data Streaming
	  	^																^
		|																|
		|																|
	Governance and Security    (Table ACLs)    (Data warehouses' security and governance models)   
	  2. Incompatible security and governance models           		Governance and Security   (Files and Blobs)
		^																^
		|																|
	Data Warehouse  (Structured tables)   							Data Lake   (unstructured files: logs, text, images, video,     )                 
	  3. Disjointed and duplicative data silos    
 ===========
	Data Lakehouses implement similar data structures and data management features as data warehouses.                         
		Data lakehouses are also open environments built in the cloud, preventing vendor lock-in, ie. noncompetitive, aggressive, and monopolistic practices                         
			They're cheap because they use cloud storage underlying them

		Data governance and security concerns:                        
			Principles, practices, and tools to manage an organization's data assets throughout their lifecycle.                         
			This includes understanding who has access to what data, auditing that access, and understanding how that data is used in business.      
			There are 8 key elements to data governance strategy: data cataloging, data classification, auditing, data entitlements and access data discovery, data sharing and collaboration, data lineage, data security, and data quality.            

		There are several challenges when attempting to implement data governance in existing data ecosystems.  Just to name a few of them:  fragmented views of your data estate, multiple tools for access management in complete monitoring and visibility, lack of cross-platform data sharing, and all this complexity impacts the pace of innovation, increases the risk of non-compliance and security breaches, and in general leaves the data ecosystem lacking for features and functionality to properly get the most out of your data.   
		Three major areas of top concern are shown here on the screen when considering data security and governance, and that includes complying with new and rapidly evolving global AI regulation, data privacy, and protections spurred on by AI related lawsuits and the ever present cyber crime scene that has entered a new era with the assistance of AI for executing cyber attacks.                              

	This is where Unity Catalog comes in.  Unity Catalog is the unified governance model for all the data and AI assets within the Databricks data intelligence platform.  Through the catalog, Databricks seeks to simplify governance, sharing and collaboration across the enterprise by providing one governance and sharing model for data analytics and AI built right into the platform.  Unity Catalog is a unification bringing together the various governance needs of your data ecosystem under one tool.  Additionally, Unity Catalog is also available as an open source implementation.

	Key features of Unity Catalog include the defined once Secure Everywhere concept.  Unity Catalog offers a single place to administer data policies that apply across all workspaces standards, compliance, security model.  Unity Catalogs security model allows administers to grant permissions in their existing data lake using familiar syntax at the level of catalogs.  Schemas, also known as databases, tables and views built-in auditing and lineage.  Unity Catalog automatically captures user level audit logs that records access to your data.  Unity Catalog also captures lineage data that tracks how the data assets are created and used across all languages.              

	Data Discovery Unity Catalog lets you tag and document data assets and provides a search interface to help data consumers find data easily.  And System Tables Unity Catalog lets you easily access and query your accounts operational data, including audit logs, billable usage, and lineage for governance and security.  Databricks Data Intelligence platform supports several compliance standards to meet the regulatory requirements of your business, all in a single simple and unified platform governed by Unity Catalog.

		DLT is the first ETL framework that uses a simple declarative approach to building reliable data pipelines.
		DLT automatically manages your infrastructure at scale.  So data analysts and engineers can spend less time on tooling and focus on getting value form data.      
			"With DLT, engineers are able to treat their data as code and apply modern software engineering best practices like testing, error handling, monitoring and documentation to deploy reliable pipelines at scale."     to

		Once you have all your data in, now you need to make use of that data through analysis and business intelligence.  For data warehousing analytics, Databricks offers Databricks SQL.  Databricks SQL is a collection of tools for BI and analytics.  On the surface, it supports the use of ANSI SQL through a full featured SQL editing environments within the platform.  However, under the covers, Databricks SQL has been optimized specifically for SQL workloads and production environments, offering world-class price and performance.  Paired with a unified storage layer, Databricks SQL provides you with the support for both batch and streaming workloads for many enterprise level use cases and data analytics and BI.  With alerts and schedules, you're able to keep track of how your queries are performing and automatically refresh results as needed.  The best part of this is: it is all available in a single platform, keeping your data and users across teams together to avoid working in silos.  And with AI integrated directly into the platform, users can get answers quicker through the use of natural language queries.  Databricks understands that in order to support data warehousing workloads, you need to include many related and key features common among data warehousing products.  You can't have a data warehouse without materialized views, role-based access controls, and many, many more capabilities.  So Databricks built all of those into the various foundational components of the lakehouse, including multi-format support and Unity Catalog, and they exposes them through Databricks SQL for your use and benefit.  Additionally, Databricks offers proven methods and guidance as well as automatic tooling for migrating from legacy warehouses to Databricks in a fast and predictable manner.  
		And for analytics reporting and business intelligence.  Databricks offers AI BI Databricks currently has two AI BI tools to support these endeavors.  The first is AI BI dashboards, which is a built-in environment for presenting and reviewing data analytics and BI work.  With built-in AI, analysts can use natural language to select data for their dashboards and even use natural language to create new visuals in the point and click environment.  With the dataset, permissions, queries, and visualizations collected into a single space, you can easily present and share your findings with others in your Databricks account, regardless of whether or not they have specific access to your workspace.  But you can't always anticipate every question the user will want to have answered in the dashboard.  So that is why we've developed AI BI Genie.  AI BI Genie is a space where users can ask questions directly to the data itself using Natural Language prompts to garner insights and find answers to their ad hoc questions.  This essentially allows users to talk to the data, removing the analyst as the middleman between the end user and data users can ask any questions that aren't addressed directly through your regularly scheduled dashboard.  Refresh cycles, making it easy to support multiple use cases.  And we understand that it may be tough for business analysts and data practitioners to move off of their favorite BI and visualization tool, which is why Databricks offers a couple of deep integrations for tools that you may know and love Power BI and Tableau.  With a click, you can connect your Power BI or Tableau accounts to Databricks to for you to access tables and views held in Unity Catalog directly from Tableau and Power BI.  Unity Catalog acts as a unification point and you can still use the tools and interfaces that you're familiar with to develop and present your findings to stakeholders in workflows you've already established.  And finally, what about data science, machine learning, and generative AI?

		Serving acts as a catalyst for getting models to production in a typical ML or Gen AI model, life cycle model serving is tightly integrated with model registry.  The model registry allows efficient management and staging of models while model serving facilitates deployment of any version of the model through rest endpoints, providing flexibility and ease of use for real-time applications.  And all of this is based on the implementation of ml Flow through the Databricks platform.  Now, when we speak about generative AI, most people think of general intelligence models, which are LLMs available for use on the web.  These general intelligence models are good for writing a college essay paper or planning your next vacation, but those aren't use cases for enterprise data environments.  Enterprise businesses need AI that can solve specific problems and handle unique tasks for their business, and that is why Mosaic AI is the best tool for the job when it comes to implementing Gen AI use cases on the enterprise scale to get accurate domain specific results for your business.  This often involves creating systems that rely on gen AI-based agents.  And when there are many models on the market, it can be difficult to determine what's best for your business, especially when what's best for your business depends on the task at hand and the models can change every week.  So instead of wasting time determining which model to use, Databricks makes it quick and efficient to evaluate models and implement them in production.  We support every foundational model on the market and make it easy to tune them with your data, and you can compare multiple models over time to swap in new models.

		As the market evolves, you're never locked into a cloud or provider model for anything, and most importantly, you have the tools to evaluate and monitor models in production to ensure they're performing as intended.  Now that we have an idea of what features and tools are available within the Databricks data intelligence platform, let's talk about that data.  Databricks is built to support the data format you choose in an open source based storage layer.  This storage layer enhances data lakes by adding Reliability, performance, governance, and quality.  It achieves this by implementing a transactional layer on top of the open Parquet, Iceberg, or Delta Data format, enabling the Lakehouse paradigm that combines the strengths of data lakes with the strengths of data warehouses.  This storage layer allows you to unify your data cross your entire ecosystem in one location, allowing you to break free from vendor specific formats.  You can control your own data and avoid unnecessary duplication across multiple platforms, which in turn impact the expense of storing and managing your data assets.  Additionally, we offer Delta sharing.  Delta sharing is an open cross-platform sharing tool easily allowing you to share existing data in Delta, Iceberg, or Parquet formats without having to establish new ingestion processes to consume data.  Data is shared live without copying it, with data being maintained on that provider's data lake, ensuring the data sets are reliable in real time, and provide the most current information to the data recipient.  Delta Sharing provides centralized Administration and governance to the data provider as the data is governed, tracked, and audited from a single location, allowing usage to be monitored at the table, partition, and version level.  With Delta sharing, you can build and package data products through a central marketplace for distribution to anywhere and a safe and secure with privacy safe data.  

		Clean rooms, meaning collaboration between data providers and recipients is hosted in a secure environment while safeguarding data privacy as part of Unity Catalog.  Delta sharing provides platform independent sharing and collaboration, resulting in a reduced total cost of ownership for the data ecosystem by removing the need to duplicate data.  Additionally, businesses can unlock new potential revenue and business value through avenues such as the Databricks marketplace, which is backed by Delta Sharing.    Speaking of Databricks marketplace, it's our open marketplace for data analytics and AI that brings together a vast ecosystem of data consumers and data providers to collaborate across a wide array of data sets without platform dependencies, complicated ETL, expensive replication, or vendor lock in, powered by Delta sharing and backed by Unity Catalog.
		Data providers can expand their business and user reach while unlocking new revenue opportunities as an open marketplace.  Databricks marketplace can be used for exchanging data analytics and AI products such as datasets, notebooks, dashboards, and machine learning models.  And it does not rely on the recipient having a Databricks workspace either, making it an open exchange space for all types of data products.  If you're interested in trying out our marketplace, check out the data and AI summit cookies dataset, a popular dataset that can be used to illustrate several different demonstrations throughout the platform.  And because Databricks is built on open source and open standards, it provides a core platform for your ecosystem integrating with the tools you use today, and the new technologies you might adopt tomorrow.  

		And finally, where are data practitioners supposed  to do all of this work?  Well, that would be the Databricks Workspace.  The workspace is a user-friendly environment that offers several benefits to Databricks users.  The workspace Homepage provides shortcuts to common tasks across the entire platform, including importing data, creating notebooks, queries, and configuring auto ML experiments and more, giving you a centralized place to access all your data and development objects.  You can also access intelligent search right from the home screen to locate data objects throughout the platform.  Databricks notebook are one of the core components of the workspace for all data practitioners within the platform.  With support for Python, SQL, and Scala, you'll be able to work efficiently and effectively within the interface.   

		And with Databricks assistant in the wings, you'll have access to troubleshooting support any time of the day, if your code isn't executing as planned.  Catalog explorer is where you'll find the details of your data catalogs, including all information about schemas, tables, views, volumes, and more.  You can browser and manage all the data details, including adding descriptions and details to the tables and columns for better data discovery.             
		And with Full Data Lineage, you'll be able to track how the table was created, and how it's being used throughout your data ecosystem.  Catalog  explorer is your window to Unity Catalog. 	ReactJS, full course (https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RVFAyFWO4go&ab_channel=DaveGray):                     
		Notes, random thoughts, etc.  :                     
			1.  Install Node.js.       
			2.  package.json has versions of dependencies, and script names (ie. "`npm start`", "`npm build`", etc.   )    
			3.	"// Modern React uses "Functional Components" .    ("Class components" no longer used as of React 16.8)
//  This is a functional component, which is a JavaScript function that returns JSX."
			  a.
			4.
			5.  Promise:					
				a.  A `Promise` is an object representing the eventual completion (or failure) of an asynchronous operation and its resulting value.    
				b.  It provides a cleaner way to handle asynchronous operations compared to traditional callback functions, helping to avoid "callback hell" by allowing for chaining of operations using `.then()` and `.catch()`.   
				c.  A Promise can be in one of three states:   `pending`, `fulfilled` (resolved successfully), or `rejected` (failed)  .    

			6.  async:                    The async keyword is used to declare a function is asynchronous and contains await keywords?  Or just other functions that wait on a different) event to complete before proceeding with the normal, sequential,  declarative order of operating.   .      
				a.  The `async` keyword is used to declare a function as asynchronous.   
				b.  An `async` function implicitly returns a `Promise`.  The value returned by an `async` funtcion is wrapped in a resolved `Promise`.  If an `async` function throws an error, the `Promise` it returns will be rejected.   
			7.  await:
				a.  The `await` keyword can only be used inside an `async` function.      
				b.  It pauses the execution of the `async` function until the `Promise` it is waiting on settles (either fulfills or rejects).   j  
				c.  When the `Promise` fulfills, `await` returns the resolved value of the `Promise`.  If the `Promise` rejects, `await` throws an error, which can be caught using a `try...catch` block.   
			8.  Relationships and usage:
				a.  `async/awit` is essentially syntactic sugar built on top of `Promises`.  It provides a more synchronous-looking and readable way to write asynchronous code, making it easier to manage complex asynchronous flows.
				b.  You use `Promises` directly when you need more fine-grained control over the asynchronous operation's lifecycle or when working with older asynchronous APIs that return Promises.   
				c.  You use `async / await` when you want to simplify the handling of Promises and make asynchronous code more sequential and easier to reason about, especially when dealing with multiple sequential asynchronous operations.   
			9.
			10.
			11.
			12.
			13.
			14.

	An example of the promises chaining:                      

fetchPromise
  .then((response) => {
    if (!response.ok) {
      throw new Error(`HTTP error: ${response.status}`);
    }
    return response.json();
  })
  .then((data) => {
    console.log(data[0].name);
  })
  .catch((error) => {
    console.error(`Could not get products: ${error}`);
  });

	await and async     example:                       

async function fetchProducts() {
  try {
    // after this line, our function will wait for the `fetch()` call to be settled
    // the `fetch()` call will either return a Response or throw an error
    const response = await fetch(
      "https://mdn.github.io/learning-area/javascript/apis/fetching-data/can-store/products.json",
    );
    if (!response.ok) {
      throw new Error(`HTTP error: ${response.status}`);
    }
    // after this line, our function will wait for the `response.json()` call to be settled
    // the `response.json()` call will either return the parsed JSON object or throw an error
    const data = await response.json();
    console.log(data[0].name);
  } catch (error) {
    console.error(`Could not get products: ${error}`);
  }
}

fetchProducts();

	Summary of NDA clause:  		 "What You **__ARE__** Legally Allowed to Discuss:                               
The clause specifies certain things that are not considered confidential information, and you are therefore allowed to discuss them. These include:

Sexual assault or sexual harassment claims: The agreement explicitly states that you are free to discuss or disclose details related to these claims.

General knowledge and skills: Any general knowledge, skills, and experience you acquire during your employment are not considered confidential. This means you can't be prevented from using your general professional abilities in the future.

Publicly available information: Information that was already publicly known or became publicly known after you learned it (as long as it wasn't due to your wrongful actions).

Information you possessed beforehand: Information that you had in your rightful possession, without any confidentiality obligations, before the company disclosed it to you.

What You Are NOT Legally Allowed to Discuss
The clause is very broad and aims to protect any non-public information that has value to the company. Therefore, you are not legally allowed to discuss anything that falls under the definition of "Company Confidential Information." This means you should be extremely cautious about discussing work details with your spouse, friends, family, or anyone else, even in private settings like over the dinner table.

Given the comprehensive list of confidential information (product plans, customer lists, finances, technology, etc.), it is safest to assume that any specific, non-public detail about your work or the company's operations is off-limits for discussion.

Disclaimer: This is a summary based on the text you provided and is for informational purposes only. It is not legal advice. For a definitive legal interpretation and guidance, you should consult with an attorney."			Wealthsmart, Inc.

	Page 7, subsection 3B.  B, "Pre-Existing Materials" :                     
Hello Nate, that's an excellent question that gets to the heart of intellectual property agreements. Here is a breakdown of how these two clauses work together.

The two parts of the agreement you've highlighted cover different types of inventions.
Section A deals with inventions you create during your employment at the company. This clause states that all such work belongs entirely to the company.
Section B deals with Pre-Existing Materials or "Prior Inventions"—that is, intellectual property you owned before you started working for the company. You are required to disclose these if you plan to use them in your work.

The crucial difference is in ownership. For any invention created during your employment, you give up all ownership to the company. For your prior inventions, you maintain ownership. Instead of owning the invention, the company is granted a nonexclusive, royalty-free, perpetual, irrevocable, worldwide license to use it.

The "Worldwide License" and Your Ability to Earn Fees:         
	The term nonexclusive is key here. Because the license is nonexclusive, you are free to license your prior inventions to other companies, including for a fee. The company (Boosted, in this case) receives its own license to use your prior invention at no cost, which is what the "royalty-free" part of the agreement states.         
	Therefore, for your prior inventions, you are not giving Boosted all rights. You can still use those inventions for your own benefit, including licensing them to other parties for a fee, but you cannot charge Boosted a fee for using them.                  

	I cannot claim ownership of inventions I make during my tenure at the company.  ie. I can't demand I be credited with my name on things during the course of my work here, etc.,    attributed                     
	  What are "Moral Rights"? Moral rights are personal rights granted to the creator of a work, separate from the economic rights (like the right to sell or reproduce the work). The most common moral rights are:
	  Right of Attribution: The right to be named as the author or creator of your work.            
	  Right of Integrity: The right to prevent unauthorized modifications or distortions of your work that could damage your reputation.            

I agree to keep and maintain adequate, current, accurate, and authentic written records of all Inventions made by me (solely or jointly with others) during the term of my employment with the Company. The records will be in the form of notes, sketches, drawings, electronic files, reports, or any other format that may be specified by the Company. As between the Company and myself, the records are and will be available to and remain the sole property of the Company at all times.

Section F: Attorney-in-Fact
	This clause grants the company the power to act on your behalf if you become unavailable. It states that if you are unable to sign documents to secure the company's rights to an invention—due to an illness, absence, or any other reason—the company can sign on your behalf. This authorization is an "irrevocable power of attorney," meaning the company can use it without your consent and even after you've left your employment.      This is a clause I actually like.  I'm not giving away money or time in this one, lol.                      

Section G: Exception to Assignments
	This clause outlines a narrow exception to the general rule that the company owns all your work. It states that you may retain ownership of an invention you developed entirely on your own time, using your own resources, as long as the invention is not related to the company's business, its research and development, or any work you performed for the company. If, however, you choose to incorporate any of these personal inventions into a company product or service, you must first get the company's permission and then grant the company a broad, royalty-free, perpetual license to use it.

Page 11, subsection 8d:   
	This is an excellent question that gets to the heart of how non-compete clauses are enforced. This section does more than just apply the non-compete universally; it's a legal safeguard for the company.

	This "Separate Covenants" clause of the noncompete is called a severability clause or blue-penciling provision. It states that if a court finds any part of the non-compete or non-solicitation clauses to be unenforceable (for example, if the geographic area is too broad), the court has the authority to reform the clause to be legally enforceable. This means that instead of throwing out the entire non-compete, a court could simply reduce the scope (e.g., shrink the geographic area from the entire U.S. to just Virginia) to a level that is considered reasonable and lawful.

	You are correct that the initial intent is to apply the covenants across all territories where the company operates, but the more significant part of this clause is its ability to be modified by a court to become enforceable. It ensures that the company will still have some level of protection, even if the original terms are deemed too restrictive.

	You don't need to do anything to obey this clause, as it pertains to how the agreement would be handled in a legal dispute. It's a provision for the company's benefit in a worst-case scenario.

You are right to be attentive to this section, as it outlines the company's comprehensive monitoring rights and your responsibilities regarding technology use.

Here is a summary of the clauses and a breakdown of your questions:

Summary
This clause states that you have no reasonable expectation of privacy on any company-owned electronic media or systems. All information on these systems is the property of the company. The company has the right to audit, monitor, and access any and all of this information for business-related purposes, at its sole discretion, and without notice to you. This includes not only company devices but also your personal devices if you use them for company business.

How They Will Be Monitoring You
The document explicitly outlines the company's monitoring capabilities, which include:

Auditing and Searching: The company can audit and search all company-owned systems.

Network Traffic Monitoring: The company has or may acquire software to monitor and record all network traffic.

Accessing Communications: They can access, review, copy, and delete all sent and received emails, voicemails, and messages.

Internet Activity: They can monitor all website visits, chat sessions, and any information you download.

File Transfers: They can monitor all file transfers into and out of the company's network.

Retrieving Deleted Items: They can retrieve previously deleted emails and voicemails.

What You Are Not Allowed to Do

No Unlicensed Software: You are not permitted to add any "unlicensed, unauthorized, or non-compliant applications," including free or open-source software not authorized by the company. ===================================================================================================
Hello Nate, I can certainly help you understand this final section.

Summary:

This clause requires you to agree to mandatory, binding arbitration for almost any legal dispute you may have with the company. It applies to a broad range of claims, including those related to discrimination, harassment, wrongful termination, and contract disputes. By agreeing to this, you are explicitly waiving your right to a jury trial and your ability to participate in or initiate a class-action lawsuit against the company. The clause also outlines the specific procedures for this arbitration, including that it will be administered by JAMS under Virginia law and that you may be required to share the costs.
What You Can and Cannot Do

What you can do:

You can bring individual claims against the company through the arbitration process.

You can pursue administrative claims with government agencies like the EEOC or NLRB, though you are barred from then taking that claim to court.

You can still sue the company for injunctive relief related to a breach of the NDA (e.g., to stop them from using your confidential information).

What you cannot do:

You cannot sue the company in a court of law for most employment-related disputes.

You cannot participate in or initiate a class-action lawsuit.

You cannot demand a jury trial for disputes covered by the agreement.

What Legal Rights You Still Have
While this clause is very restrictive, you still have some legal rights:

The right to bring a claim to a neutral, third-party arbitrator.

The ability to seek remedies that are available under applicable law, as determined by the arbitrator.

The right to pursue claims with administrative bodies (e.g., the EEOC).

The right to a court hearing for a claim for injunctive relief.

The clause notes that it does not apply to claims that cannot be arbitrated under applicable law, such as certain claims under the Sarbanes-Oxley Act.

Now that we have covered the key clauses of your NDA and created a summary checklist of your obligations, what is your biggest remaining concern about the document?

Hello again, I'm doing well, thank you! I can see that you are being very thorough in your review, and I'm happy to help you finish strong.

Here is a summary of these final clauses and an explanation of your rights.

Summary
Section D (Availability of Injunctive Relief): This clause provides an important exception to the binding arbitration rule. It states that either you or the company can go directly to court to get an "injunction" to stop a violation of the NDA (such as a breach of non-compete, non-solicitation, or a disclosure of confidential information). The clause specifies that such a breach would cause "irreparable injury," meaning that a simple payment of money would not be an adequate remedy. It also states that the party who wins in this type of court action can recover their legal fees.

Section E (Administrative Relief): This clause clarifies that while you cannot sue the company in court for most claims, you are not prohibited from filing a complaint with a government agency, such as the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (EEOC) or the National Labor Relations Board (NLRB).

Your Rights
Rights You Have: You gain the specific right to seek an injunction in court to stop a breach of the contract, which is a powerful tool to prevent ongoing harm. You also explicitly retain your right to file claims with state or federal administrative agencies.

Rights You Are Waiving: These clauses do not waive any new rights. Rather, they serve as a clarification and exception to the much broader waiver you made in the previous arbitration clause (Section 12.A), which is where you gave up your right to a jury trial and class-action lawsuits for most disputes.

Section A (Governing Law; Consent to Personal Jurisdiction): This clause establishes that the agreement will be legally governed by and interpreted under Virginia law. It also requires you to consent to any lawsuits being filed exclusively in state or federal courts in Virginia. This means if a lawsuit is permitted, you cannot file it in your home state (if it is not Virginia).

Section B (Waiver of Trial by Jury): This is a critical clause. It explicitly and irrevocably states that you are waiving your right to a trial by jury for any lawsuit related to this agreement. This reinforces the arbitration clause and ensures that even in cases that might go to court, a judge will decide the outcome, not a jury.

Section C (Assignability): This clause makes the agreement binding on your heirs and legal representatives. More importantly, it gives the company the right to transfer or "assign" the entire agreement to a new owner. This means that if the company is sold, merged, or acquired, the new company can legally enforce all the terms of this NDA against you.

More summary of a few lines at once:                      :                     

Section 13.D (Entire Agreement): This clause, also known as a merger clause, states that this document is the final and complete agreement between you and the company. It means you cannot rely on any promises or discussions that were made during interviews or negotiations if they are not written in this document. Any changes to your job or salary will not affect the validity of this NDA.

Section 13.E (Headings): This is a standard provision clarifying that the section titles (like "Entire Agreement") are for organizational purposes only and do not affect the legal meaning of the text.

Section 13.F (Severability): This clause ensures that if a part of the agreement is found to be legally invalid, the rest of the agreement will still hold up. The invalid part will be modified or removed, allowing the rest of the contract to be enforced.
		I HATE this Severability clause.  This means that even though they didn't tell me to get an attorney to look over this document, I am still restricted by the rest of the document.                         
Section 13.G (Modification, Waiver): This states that the agreement can only be changed with a formal written amendment signed by both you and a company executive. It also clarifies that the company not enforcing a rule once doesn't mean they've waived their right to enforce it in the future.

Section 13.H (Survivorship): This is a critical point: the obligations and rights outlined in the NDA (such as confidentiality and non-compete) do not end when your employment does. They will "survive" the termination of your job.

Section 14 (Protected Activity Not Prohibited): This is an important section that protects your legal rights. It clarifies that this agreement does not prevent you from filing a complaint or cooperating with a government agency (like the EEOC or SEC). It also notes that you have the right to discuss your wages, terms, and conditions of employment, as protected by law, and that you are immune from liability for limited disclosures of trade secrets as outlined in the Defend Trade Secrets Act.

Exhibit B: Termination Certification
This is a form you will have to sign when your employment ends. By signing it, you are certifying that you have returned all company property and confidential information. You also certify that you have updated your social media to reflect that you are no longer an employee and that you have complied with the non-compete and non-solicitation clauses.

Exhibit C: Conflict of Interest Guidelines
This is a list of common situations you must avoid to prevent conflicts of interest. It reinforces the main agreement's principles, such as not misusing confidential information and avoiding excessive gifts. It also explicitly prohibits you from actions like investing in or holding a directorship at competing companies, using trade secrets from a former employer, or engaging in any conduct not in the company's best interest. It also reiterates your rights to discuss employment terms with other employees and file complaints with government agencies, while still maintaining confidentiality.

Exhibit D: Defend Trade Secrets Act of 2016
This exhibit provides you with a required legal notice from the Defend Trade Secrets Act. It grants you immunity from legal liability for disclosing a trade secret to a government official or an attorney, but only for the purpose of reporting or investigating a suspected violation of the law. It also allows you to use a trade secret in a lawsuit against your employer for retaliation, as long as it is done under seal and with a court order.

Para información en español, visite www.consumerfinance.gov/learnmore o escribe a la Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, 1700 G Street NW, Washington, DC 20552.

A Summary of Your Rights Under the Fair Credit Reporting Act

The federal Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA) promotes the accuracy, fairness, and privacy of information in the files of consumer reporting agencies. There are many types of consumer reporting agencies, including credit bureaus and specialty agencies (such as agencies that sell information about check writing histories, medical records, and rental history records). Here is a summary of your major rights under FCRA. For more information, including information about additional rights, go to www.consumerfinance.gov/learnmore or write to: Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, 1700 G Street NW, Washington, DC 20552.

You must be told if information in your file has been used against you. Anyone who uses a credit report or another type of consumer report to deny your application for credit, insurance, or employment – or to take another adverse action against you – must tell you, and must give you the name, address, and phone number of the agency that provided the information.

You have the right to know what is in your file. You may request and obtain all the information about you in the files of a consumer reporting agency (your “file disclosure”). You will be required to provide proper identification, which may include your Social Security number. In many cases, the disclosure will be free. You are entitled to a free file disclosure if:

a person has taken adverse action against you because of information in your credit report;

you are the victim of identity theft and place a fraud alert in your file;

your file contains inaccurate information as a result of fraud;

you are on public assistance;

you are unemployed but expect to apply for employment within 60 days.

In addition, all consumers are entitled to one free disclosure every 12 months upon request from each nationwide credit bureau and from nationwide specialty consumer reporting agencies. See www.consumerfinance.gov/learnmore for additional information.

You have the right to ask for a credit score. Credit scores are numerical summaries of your credit-worthiness based on information from credit bureaus. You may request a credit score from consumer reporting agencies that create scores or distribute scores used in residential real property loans, but you will have to pay for it. In some mortgage transactions, you will receive credit score information for free from the mortgage lender.

You have the right to dispute incomplete or inaccurate information. If you identify information in your file that is incomplete or inaccurate, and report it to the consumer reporting agency, the agency must investigate unless your dispute is frivolous. See www.consumerfinance.gov/learnmore for an explanation of dispute procedures.

Consumer reporting agencies must correct or delete inaccurate, incomplete, or unverifiable information. Inaccurate, incomplete, or unverifiable information must be removed or corrected, usually within 30 days. However, a consumer reporting agency may continue to report information it has verified as accurate.

Consumer reporting agencies may not report outdated negative information. In most cases, a consumer reporting agency may not report negative information that is more than seven years old, or bankruptcies that are more than 10 years old.

Access to your file is limited. A consumer reporting agency may provide information about you only to people with a valid need – usually to consider an application with a creditor, insurer, employer, landlord, or other business. The FCRA specifies those with a valid need for access.

You must give your consent for reports to be provided to employers. A consumer reporting agency may not give out information about you to your employer, or a potential employer, without your written consent given to the employer. Written consent generally is not required in the trucking industry. For more information, go to www.consumerfinance.gov/learnmore.

You may limit “prescreened” offers of credit and insurance you get based on information in your credit report. Unsolicited “prescreened” offers for credit and insurance must include a toll-free phone number you can call if you choose to remove your name and address from the lists these offers are based on. You may opt out with the nationwide credit bureaus at 1-888-567-8688.

The following FCRA right applies with respect to nationwide consumer reporting agencies:

CONSUMERS HAVE THE RIGHT TO OBTAIN A SECURITY FREEZE
You have a right to place a “security freeze” on your credit report, which will prohibit a consumer reporting agency from releasing information in your credit report without your express authorization. The security freeze is designed to prevent credit, loans, and services from being approved in your name without your consent. However, you should be aware that using a security freeze to take control over who gets access to the personal and financial information in your credit report may delay, interfere with, or prohibit the timely approval of any subsequent request or application you make regarding a new loan, credit, mortgage, or any other account involving the extension of credit.

As an alternative to a security freeze, you have the right to place an initial or extended fraud alert on your credit file at no cost. An initial fraud alert is a 1-year alert that is placed on a consumer’s credit file. Upon seeing a fraud alert display on a consumer’s credit file, a business is required to take steps to verify the consumer’s identity before extending new credit. If you are a victim of identity theft, you are entitled to an extended fraud alert, which is a fraud alert lasting 7 years.

A security freeze does not apply to a person or entity, or its affiliates, or collection agencies acting on behalf of the person or entity, with which you have an existing account that requests information in your credit report for the purposes of reviewing or collecting the account. Reviewing the account includes activities related to account maintenance, monitoring, credit line increases, and account upgrades and enhancements.

You may seek damages from violators. If a consumer reporting agency, or, in some cases, a user of consumer reports or a furnisher of information to a consumer reporting agency violates the FCRA, you may be able to sue in state or federal court.

Identity theft victims and active duty military personnel have additional rights. For more information, visit www.consumerfinance.gov/learnmore.

States may enforce the FCRA, and many states have their own consumer reporting laws. In some cases, you may have more rights under state law. For more information, contact your state or local consumer protection agency or your state Attorney General. For information about your federal rights, contact:

TYPE OF BUSINESS:	CONTACT:
